- Verifying concurrent software using movers in Cspec (2018)
    - what: Cspec is a Coq framework for implementing and proving concurrent systems through state-machine refinement, composable layers and reusable proof patterns. Right/left movers reduce interleavings to atomic execution of larger code blocks. Fully mechanized patterns verify a Maildir-like server, Cmail, and a locked counter over an x86-TSO model. Extracted Haskell Cmail scales on a twelve-core tmpfs benchmark. Records state/argument-dependent commutation and stable enablement, finite-prefix safety scope, protocol proof obligations, error-state elimination, zero-effect retries and partition lifting. Captures six concrete mail concurrency cases, conservative PID-filtered directory listing, two naming plans, all TSO layers and the previous-owner buffer invariant. Saves all framework/layer LOC and pattern counts, measured setup, development anecdotes and precise trusted boundaries.
    - limits: §§1,5,7: Programs, operation/state definitions, implementations, abstraction relations, invariants and proofs are written manually within Cspec/Coq; it cannot directly verify arbitrary existing software. Executables use Coq extraction to Haskell (OCaml is mentioned as an alternative). The result is not a proof of arbitrary compiled C, Rust or a native binary. §§5.2,7: Correctness is trace inclusion, with every finite prefix representing possibly infinite behavior. Only safety properties are targeted. No scheduler fairness, termination, starvation freedom, eventual successful delivery or lock acquisition follows. The proof may insert a never-executed stably enabled left-mover into an equivalent execution; it does not make the actual scheduler run it. §§1–2,8.6: Node failures are outside scope. File-system crash behavior and durability are not proved despite the Maildir motivation. Top-level intended behavior and bottom-level file-system model must be correct. Coq soundness and standard assumptions including functional extensionality, extraction/runtime, driver and concrete syscall mapping are trusted. §§1,5.3,8: SMTP/POP3 parsing and protocol implementation are unverified. The Haskell network/file-system driver lies outside the proof. Compatibility with Go SMTP, postal and rabid is testing, not protocol conformance proof. TLS, spam filtering and logging are absent. §6.1 Figures 10–12: Commutation must preserve both operations’ individual return values and final state across distinct PIDs. Atomic blocks have right-movers, at most one non-mover, then left-movers. State-dependent properties need only hold after the already-executed prefix but require proofs for all reachable modeled states, not sampled interleavings. §6.1: Disabled intermediate operations require stable enablement when moving an operation earlier: enablement survives undoing another process’s step. An existing locked-file read can be a left-mover although reading arbitrary newly created/temporary files is not. Lock ownership rules must ensure other processes cannot release this process’s lock. §6.2: Protocol semantics disables forbidden operations, such as unlocked accesses. Sound use requires proving every API user follows the protocol under unrestricted semantics. Merely restricting the environment would assume away the race; Cspec requires the refinement proof. §6.3 Figure 13: The abstraction pattern keeps operation types fixed, preserves the return value and relates successor states for every low-level step. Invariant introduction uses an identity relation. An absorbing abstract error state can relate to any low-level state, but a later protocol proof must show it unreachable before obtaining the final guarantee. §6.4: Retry-loop bodies must either perform the correct successful effect and exit, or have zero effect and retry. This supports a safety/atomicity proof without guaranteeing eventual success. Partition lifting requires proven single-shard refinement plus correct naming and disjoint decomposition at top and bottom. §§3–5 Figures 1,3,6: The displayed mail/file-system specifications permit a false delivery/link return with no effect, not solely a proved name-collision cause. They are permissive safety specifications and do not guarantee success. The paper uses atomic rename terminology later; earlier implementation/spec uses link followed by unlink, with no destination overwrite, so do not substitute unrestricted POSIX rename semantics. §8.1: With concurrent deletion, PID-filtered readdir deliberately permits a superset of existing names. It is a right-mover, not a both-mover, in that case. A name absent from the superset is free; a reported name need not still exist. Plan 2 describes random names but concretely uses the current timestamp and relies on retry rather than guaranteed collision freedom. §8.2 Figure 16: The x86-TSO case verifies a modeled counter built over shared memory and per-core store buffers. Any core may flush any portion of its buffer at each low-level operation. This is not validation of a physical processor, compiler memory model or executable counter benchmark. §8.3 Figure 17: Performance uses local simulated SMTP/POP3 requests on Linux tmpfs, avoiding network and persistent-storage bottlenecks. Authors implemented the unverified GoMail comparator. Request mix is 1:1 delivery versus read/delete over 100 random users. Message sizes, measurement durations, repetitions, error bars and raw runs are not supplied. §8.4 Figure 18: Effort is two people about six months part time, following another twelve months of failed alternative designs. Incremental deletion/naming/multi-user timings are author anecdotes, not controlled studies or independent replications. The text’s approximate 100–200 spec LOC per layer omits displayed extremes 63 and 323. §8.5 Figure 19: Proof-pattern counts are module uses and need not equal numbers of layers. The Cmail diagram describes naming plan 1; the count table also includes the retry pattern used in plan 2, which has one additional intermediate layer. §§2,8.6: The state-machine approach draws on TLA and I/O automata, but the work is not a TLA+ implementation/proof or a distributed node-failure theorem. No Verus, verified Rust or LLM-generated-specification/proof evaluation. Public artifact URL is recorded but not fetched or replayed.

- XRP Ledger Consensus Protocol analysis (2018)
    - title: Analysis of the XRP Ledger Consensus Protocol
    - what: Gives a formal network model and safety/liveness analysis of XRP Ledger Consensus Protocol (XRP LCP), a Byzantine agreement protocol operating with individually chosen trusted node lists (UNLs). Defines nodes, asymmetric UNLs, quorum qi=ceil(.8ni), tolerated faults ti, ledgers and atomic-broadcast properties. It decomposes XRP LCP into deliberation, validation and preferred-branch components and provides pseudocode. Lemmas and propositions derive overlap conditions for ledger agreement: validation propagation through honest-majority support yields Theorem 8’s pairwise overlap bound. A constructed 102-node example shows that even 99% overlap can get stuck; Lemma 10 and Theorem 11 establish progress for a single agreed UNL with leaf validators under bounded-delay/no-fault rounds.
    - number: Theorem 8 safety condition: O_i,j > n_j/2 + n_i − q_i + t_i,j for every pair
    - number: with 80% quorums and 20% faults, roughly >90% UNL overlap is required
    - number: Example 9 demonstrates no progress despite 99% overlap and zero Byzantine faults
    - number: Theorem 11 guarantees no stuck state for a single agreed UNL of size at least k with q=n−floor((n−1)/k) plus leaves
    - limits: Safety is intended under arbitrary message asynchrony but assumes authenticated reliable channels and computationally bounded adversaries. Liveness only assumes eventual civil network delivery and no faulty nodes; FLP rules out full-asynchrony progress. Early analysis uses Byzantine accountability, explicitly rejected as tenuous; the broad theorem treats arbitrary deliberation outcomes, making the overlap condition sufficient rather than necessary. Results concern protocol rules and parameterized trust topology, not a measured deployment.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- Developing Bug-Free Machine Learning Systems With Formal Mathematics (2017)
    - what: Presents Certigrad, an implementation of stochastic computation graphs in Lean, with a reported machine-checkable proof that stochastic backpropagation produces unbiased estimates of the mathematical gradient of expected cost. It demonstrates a workflow in which formal specifications and attempted proofs guide implementation and expose both code errors and missing assumptions. It also reports verified Gaussian integration/reparameterization optimizations and an MNIST variational-autoencoder experiment with performance comparable to TensorFlow. Separates an executable stochastic program from its mathematical expectation semantics using Dist n, built by primitive sampling, deterministic returns and composition. The paper gives expectation and RNG-threading equations for each constructor. The initial all-graphs unbiasedness specification is strengthened with WellFormed, GradsExist, IntegralsExist and CanDiffUnderInts preconditions. A simple single-parameter, nonmemoizing implementation is proved first; a multi-parameter memoizing implementation is then proved equivalent. A heuristic tactic proves model-specific preconditions for the transformed AEVB graph. The verified optimization sequence integrates Gaussian KL terms and replaces parameter-dependent Gaussian sampling with standard-normal sampling followed by scale/shift, preserving the objective while changing the gradient estimator.
    - limits: Correctness is relative to the stated mathematical specification and adopted axioms. The authors assume real numbers, integration and primitive mathematical laws rather than constructing their foundations. They report repairing initially unsound axioms by restoring integrability conditions, but this reading does not independently audit that axiom set. The final displayed scalar theorem requires unique node identifiers and scalar leaves (WellFormed), appropriate differentiability for all relevant stochastic choices (GradsExist), existence of integrals (IntegralsExist), and conditions allowing gradients through integrals (CanDiffUnderInts). The implementation is reported to handle arbitrary tensor shapes and multiple parameters; the displayed specification deliberately simplifies these. Proving the generic algorithm is insufficient for an arbitrary client graph: each graph must meet the preconditions. Some are undecidable in general, so the theorem-proving tactic is heuristic rather than a complete decision procedure. The proof concerns ideal infinite-precision real arithmetic. The authors explicitly say replacing reals with floating-point numbers technically invalidates the specification and may cause numerical instability; no floating-point error bound is established here. The running system trusts optimized Eigen tensor operations and a small C++ wrapper to match the formally reasoned operations, and relies on Lean VM/runtime and primitive pseudo-random sampling. The paper describes corresponding mathematical/procedural distribution interpretations but does not establish a checked correctness theorem for the concrete RNG, Eigen, C++ wrapper or execution platform. Unbiased stochastic gradients do not establish convergence of ADAM, bounded gradient variance, statistical validity, good generalization or model fit. The empirical AEVB training comparison addresses one example, not those general guarantees. The source repeatedly says proving exposes all implementation errors. The supported guarantee is absence of violations of the modeled specification under its assumptions; specification errors, inconsistent axioms and implementation layers outside the model remain material, as the paper itself discusses. Claims of reduced cognitive burden and finding all bugs early are the authors’ development experience, not a controlled developer study or complete empirical bug census. The paper reports increased overall workload but supplies no person-hour or proof-size measurements.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - notes outside this site: `/ssd1/sichangheagent/verus_distributed/lit_venues/full_text_recovered/formal305/bugfree.reading.txt`
    - record id: `developing-bug-free-machine-2017`

- Girault et al. MPC (2017)
    - title: Monotonic Prefix Consistency in Distributed Systems
    - what: Defines Monotonic Prefix Consistency (MPC) for replicated lists, gives a centralized toy implementation whose traces satisfy MPC, and proves that no convergent distributed implementation under asynchronous message delivery, availability, and the paper’s observability can be strictly stronger than MPC. Formalizes implementations as total update/query/message handlers, asynchronous broadcast/receive executions with causal delivery and fairness, and traces that record per-site query order/results plus an unordered set of writes. MPC requires per-site monotone reads, pairwise prefix-compatible reads across sites, and read values drawn from writes. Lemmas establish update/query availability, invisible-read deletion, fair execution limits, and convergence extension; induction then realizes every finite MPC trace and a limit argument realizes infinite traces. The paper compares MPC with causal and strong consistency and notes its observability makes MPC and strong-consistency traces indistinguishable.
    - number: MPC monotonicity/prefix/consistency conditions
    - number: finite and infinite trace construction
    - number: eventual-consistency convergence definition
    - number: all messages eventually delivered
    - limits: Assumes broadcast communication (peer-to-peer is encoded), all messages eventually delivered in infinite executions, total handlers (availability), list updates/queries with updates split from queries, unique write values (timestamps can enforce this), and a trace abstraction that hides write origin and write/query real-time order. Convergence means every write is absent from only finitely many queries. The impossibility does not directly cover operations that are both reads and writes, origin-sensitive traces, or probabilistic settings; the MPC implementation is centralized for simplicity.

- Gomes et al. SEC Isabelle (2017)
    - title: Verifying Strong Eventual Consistency in Distributed Systems
    - what: Builds a reusable Isabelle/HOL framework that formalizes strong eventual consistency (SEC), an asynchronous unreliable causal-broadcast network, and three operation-based CRDTs: Replicated Growable Array, Observed-Remove Set, and increment-decrement Counter. The abstract convergence theorem proves that distinct happens-before-consistent operation lists with the same set of operations yield the same state when concurrent operations commute, while a separate no-failure condition gives progress. The network locale has six axioms covering unique node histories, delivery cause, local delivery, globally unique message IDs, causal delivery, and valid broadcast operations; messages may be delayed, reordered, dropped, or nodes may crash permanently. RGA proof models immutable element IDs, tombstones, insertion ordering, causal validity, and proves concurrent operations commute and application never fails. ORSet uses add IDs and remove sets of observed IDs; Add/Remove commute only under a proved independence condition. Counter operations commute unconditionally. The framework is executable via Isabelle code generation; one counter implementation ran over TCP, but detailed performance/fault-tolerance evaluation was left for future work.
    - number: 3 CRDTs verified
    - number: 6 network axioms
    - number: ~620 lines network, ~380 convergence, ~775 RGA, ~270 ORSet, ~55 Counter
    - number: ORSet proof about four hours; Counter minutes
    - limits: Network assumptions are axiomatic: finite totally ordered per-node histories, unique events/IDs, causal delivery for dependencies, and valid-msg predicates supplied by the algorithm. The model uses crash-stop permanent node failure, not recovery, and does not model overlay implementation, timing, or resource limits. RGA retains tombstones; garbage collection is explicitly out of scope. The correctness claims are for operation-based CRDTs satisfying the five SEC locale obligations, not arbitrary distributed data types. Executable extraction demonstrates computability rather than performance.

- Parameterized leader-election abstraction for FTSP (2017)
    - title: An Abstraction Technique for Parameterized Model Checking of Leader Election Protocols: Application to FTSP
    - what: Develops a simulation-preserving abstraction for parameterized timed leader-election protocols with unique identifiers and arbitrary topologies. It abstracts non-leader nodes and network edges around a shortest path to the future leader, approximates clock drift with finite schedulers, and incrementally checks FTSP root election for all graphs bounded by leader distance K. The method first maps identifiers/messages to future-root versus non-root values and relaxes irrelevant sequence/entry variables, then completes the graph outside a chosen shortest path and merges those nodes into an environment process. Simulation lemmas establish that checking the finite abstract model implies LTL properties on every topology in G_K. Approximate-synchrony scheduler S_a(Δ) over-approximates real clocks for a finite horizon derived from σ_l,σ_u,Δ. An incremental proof initializes already-converged path nodes and uses a non-interference guard to remove spurious environment messages. A NuSMV semi-algorithm finds optimal FG bounds without storing all reachable states.
    - number: synchronous K=7: N=107 in 13 minutes; includes 13×13 (169-node) and 13×13×13 (2197-node) grids
    - number: asynchronous K=5: N=63 in 65 minutes; includes 81-node 2D and 729-node 3D grids
    - number: table bounds: K=1 N=8, K=2 N=14, K=3 N=23 synchronous/25 asynchronous, K=4 N=35/39
    - number: clock-rate guarantee for N=107 with periods [29.7,30.3], corresponding to ±1% rates
    - limits: FTSP broadcasts are assumed to complete before the next send event; nodes start in arbitrary configurations and no faults occur during the recovery window. Clock periods lie in [σ_l,σ_u], and finite approximate-synchrony simulation only covers a bounded execution horizon. The abstraction over-approximates behavior, so spurious traces require guard strengthening and proof of the non-interference lemma. Results concern FTSP root convergence, not full time synchronization or failures during the checked interval; asynchronous K=5 timed out beyond that range.

- Practical verification framework for preemptive OS kernels (2016)
    - title: A Practical Verification Framework for Preemptive OS Kernels
    - what: Presents a Coq-mechanized framework that models preemptive-kernel API correctness as contextual refinement from a practical C subset, with abstract specifications, concrete interrupt-aware semantics, a relational concurrent separation logic, and proof tactics. It verifies scheduler, timer interrupt, synchronization APIs and priority-inversion freedom in commercial μC/OS-II. The framework separates application state from kernel state, gives small-step sequentially consistent semantics for task continuations and x86-8259A interrupt state, and uses a high-level language with nondeterministic/configurable scheduler relation. Relational assertions connect concrete memory, abstract state and remaining abstract command; ownership-transfer invariants account for nested interrupt levels. A soundness theorem is proved by compositional simulation. PIF is stated using original task priorities and proved for a simplified μC/OS-II mutex under no nested mutex use; verification required minor defensive changes because contextual refinement quantifies over arbitrary clients.
    - number: about 1,300 C lines verified, 63% of frequently used APIs
    - number: 216,000 lines of Coq implementation/proofs; 5.5 person-years
    - number: Coq framework package about 216,000 lines and compilation about 16 hours on 3.6 GHz/32 GB machine
    - number: proof-script to verified-C ratio about 26:1
    - limits: The machine model is tailored to Intel 8259A-style interrupts and sequential consistency; adapting other processors requires new interrupt rules. The C subset excludes function pointers and virtual memory. Contextual refinement assumes arbitrary clients, unlike original μC/OS-II precondition assumptions, motivating code checks. PIF theorem assumes no nested mutexes, no deadlock and scheduler consistency. Task creation/deletion was unfinished. Proof effort and data-structure abstraction (bitmap waiting queues) are substantial.

- Practical verification of decision-making in agent-based autonomous systems (2016)
    - what: Verifies actual Gwendolen belief-desire-intention decision code with AJPF, while abstracting continuous control and the external world into nondeterministic percepts and messages. Properties describe beliefs, goals, intentions and actions. Separate environmental hypotheses and control theorems can be combined deductively with these local results to obtain conditional whole-system claims. The property specification language gives temporal operators over belief, goal, action, intention and percept modalities. The method derives possible incoming shared beliefs by inspecting the abstraction-engine code, then explores their combinations instead of simulating a particular physical environment. Search-and-rescue examples distinguish finding a human from believing one was found, and prove a searcher/lifter result under explicit sensor, motion and message-delivery hypotheses using Logics Workbench. The satellite implementation separates reasoning R, abstraction A, continuous planning Ω and physical control Π; the authors verify R and report extra failure/abort plans discovered necessary during verification. Cruise-control agents add speed-limit goals and UK lane rules while retaining the discrete safety constraints previously proved in KeYmaera. Appendices explain abstraction and reasoning code, leader position assignment, cruise-control sensor translation and the propositional encoding used for deduction.
    - number: search property on 3×3 to 6×6 grids: 52/87/132/187 states, 31 s/1 min 38 s/4 min 48 s/14 min 6 s
    - number: three-thruster satellite property 18: 16,609 states, 1 h 18 min 42 s
    - number: x-only repair property 21: 2,754 states, 9 min 4 s
    - number: single-lane cruise property 31: 7,127 states, 6 min 57 s
    - limits: Guarantees about agent decisions do not establish physical outcomes without environmental assumptions. The explored program-state space must be finite; the propositional property language uses finite expansion of quantified shorthand. Input selection was manual, with automation left for future work. Reasoning is treated as instantaneous and incoming inputs change only at action points in the satellite abstraction. Some models exclude malicious/corrupt messages and the multi-agent satellite case excludes thruster failures for efficiency. The difficult repair proof is checked for the x thruster only; y/z variants are omitted because of state explosion. Program model checking is slow and unconstrained input combinations increase its state space. Cruise-control composition assumes that safe is computed correctly. The reported probabilistic sensor percentages in the conclusion are proposed future theorem forms, not measured reliability.

They also write a replacement C state machine for OpenSSL and prove in Frama-C that it only allows traces from a hand-written list of allowed traces. A Coq proof shows that two sides with equal handshake logs are in equal states.
    - number: 8 libraries tested, flaws in 6, ten attacks including eight impersonation attacks
    - number: replacement about 750 C lines; invariant spec about 460 lines; annotations about 900 lines
    - number: about 2 minutes per function, 30 minutes total
    - number: Eight libraries tested, serious flaws in six, ten attacks including eight impersonation attacks
    - limits: The work proves nothing about integrity or confidentiality. It does not prove that the system eventually makes progress. It covers one component, not the whole library. The human effort was not timed. The authors read the 2015 version, not the CACM version.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- Alloy MCA Verification (2015)
    - title: An Alloy Verification Model for Consensus-Based Auction Protocols
    - what: Builds a machine-readable Alloy model of Max-Consensus Auction (MCA) protocols, separating bidding and agreement mechanisms from policy choices, and checks convergence to conflict-free allocations. The model represents asynchronous bid and allocation vectors, timestamps, network topology, physical/virtual network mapping, policy predicates, and bounded message-processing traces. The authors optimize Alloy encoding by replacing ternary relations/integers with binary relations and custom value signatures. Assertions check eventual consensus across scopes; counterexamples show oscillation when bids are non-submodular and agents release/rebid subsequent items, plus denial-of-service via rebidding lost items.
    - number: 259K SAT clauses reduced to about 190K by abstraction changes
    - number: representative assertion runtime reduced from about one day to less than two hours
    - number: Result 1: consensus holds for checked policies except non-submodular utility plus release/rebid policy
    - number: Result 2 finds no consensus when rebidding on outbid items is allowed
    - limits: Finite Alloy scopes and SAT bounded analysis cannot establish unbounded correctness; checking SAT is generally exponential. Agents are intended to cooperate, and convergence requires no rebidding on already-lost items and generally submodular utilities. The attack analysis is model-level; preventing malicious rebids and larger instances are left open.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- A Conference Management System with Verified Document Confidentiality. (2014)
    - what: CoCon is a multiuser,multiconference web application whose deterministic functional kernel is specified in Isabelle and extracted to Scala. Develops bounded-deducibility(BD)security:before a release trigger,each alternative secret sequence permitted by a bound must remain compatible with the observer’s action/output sequence. Mechanizes a sufficient unwinding theorem and ten concrete confidentiality instances. End-to-end web-application confidentiality depends on informal code-generation/wrapper trust arguments. Specifies45authenticated kernel actions and a phase-based conference lifecycle;derives explicit paper/review/discussion/decision/reviewer-identity policies with role/time triggers and permitted version/count information. Gives a four-argument unwinding relation over paired states and remaining secret lists,independent/reaction/exit cases,a decreasing measure,and disjunction-based decomposition. Details a synchronizing PAP2proof and reports two person months including reusable proof infrastructure.
    - limits: Formal target is deterministic input-output kernel behavior and finite traces;observations are selected users’ action/output pairs. No distributed replication,network scheduling,crash recovery,availability/liveness or cryptographic secrecy theorem is presented. Isabelle kernel/logic,code generator,Scala compiler/runtime and external library behavior form trust boundaries. Code generation is used restrictively:terminating deterministic executable specification,no nontrivial data refinement,paper contents mapped to JVM files. Trace isomorphism is argued informally,not formally proved end-to-end. Web wrapper is trusted to invoke only exported step/initial-state operations and display outputs without extra leaks;third-party libraries must resist exploits. Timing channels and lower-level/browser attacks are excluded. Mapping abstract content to mutable JVM files is also an implementation representation boundary,not a theorem in this paper. BD is qualitative possibility-based secrecy. Every compatible alternative is treated as plausible;prior probabilities,externally known behavior patterns and high-user cooperation/Trojan-horse signaling are not generally eliminated. Arbitrary unauthorized coalitions are modeled as a fixed observer group,but authorized-user intentional disclosure is not prevented. Trigger occurrence removes the bound for that trace;it does not establish later confidentiality restoration or revocation. An existential trace witness need not preserve invisible high-level user actions,trace length or all external scheduling knowledge. This flexibility is deliberate and matters when interpreting learn-nothing claims. Confidentiality is relative to chosen value/observation filters,producer functions,trigger and bound. Document histories/identity/count policy must match intended disclosure. The ten independently proved source properties are not a stated theorem of joint secrecy for all combinations of secret sources. Unwinding is a sufficient proof method,not a complete decision procedure. Safety lemmas become automated but high-level unwinding strategies still need interaction;fully automated strategy synthesis/abstraction/model checking are future work.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-08867-9_11); notes outside this site: `/ssd1/sichangheagent/verus_distributed/lit_venues/full_text_recovered/formal325/conference.reading.txt`; notes outside this site: `lit_snowball/notes_coci5.md`

- Robotic pin configuration model checking (2014)
    - title: Engineering the Hardware/Software Interface for Robotic Platforms – A Comparison of Applied Model Checking with Prolog and Alloy
    - what: Models sensor/actuator pin-assignment configurations for an STM32F4 Discovery interface board used on self-driving miniature vehicles, and compares Prolog and Alloy model-checking workflows for finding feasible, all, and minimum-cost assignments. The authors build graph/tabular domain models, generate Prolog and Alloy specifications, and run a formal experiment over configurations up to ten pins. Alloy handles feasible/impossible checks with compact specifications, while Prolog is faster for enumerating and finding best assignments in the tested setup; the paper discusses model-generation cost, scope, and threats to validity.
    - number: configuration space exceeds 14.5 million possibilities
    - number: Alloy average 0.58 s for feasible larger configurations
    - number: Prolog 1.38 s for the largest best-assignment configuration
    - number: Alloy best/all assignment mean times 1.64/4.58 s in Table IV
    - limits: The study focuses on one board and a bounded configuration model, measures computation time only, and uses a 1.8 GHz Intel Core i7 with 4 GB RAM. Alloy's finite scopes and generated assertions require careful bit-width/scope choices; Prolog model loading can dominate. Generalization to other boards, semantic pin constraints, memory, and maintenance is future work.

    - title: An Integrated Specification and Verification Technique for Highly Concurrent Data Structures

Results concern safety/conformance and linearizability, not liveness or implementation-level Rust memory models.

The method is a semi-decision procedure with soundness/refinement techniques and evaluates Byzantine broadcast variants.

- Dialog Model Checking Fault-Tolerant Algorithms (2012)
    - title: Starting a Dialog between Model Checking and Fault-tolerant Distributed Algorithms?
    - what: Introduces a parameterized modeling framework for threshold-guarded fault-tolerant message-passing algorithms. Processes are extended control-flow automata with symbolic parameters, threshold guards, asynchronous interleavings, message buffers and faulty-process nondeterminism; instances become Kripke structures checked in LTL without next. The paper makes the formalization problem explicit (pseudocode semantics and hidden environmental assumptions), defines parameterized process skeletons and CFA operational semantics, and translates a family of broadcast algorithms under Byzantine, symmetric, omission and crash faults. Spin/Promela experiments systematically vary resilience and specifications: expected resilience boundaries are recovered, and impossible configurations produce violations.
    - number: Byzantine n>3t; symmetric/omission n>2t; clean crash n>t
    - number: valid cases Spin-verified; n=7,t=3 Byzantine relay specification violates as expected
    - number: n=5 omission with three faulty processes violates C and R
    - number: n=11 experiments exhaust memory
    - limits: Experiments are bounded finite instances and do not establish parameterized correctness; n=11 runs out of memory. The framework initially targets asynchronous threshold algorithms, with partial synchrony and round models left for future work. Correctness depends on explicit resilience, delay, fault and scheduling assumptions.

- ownership authentication transfer protocol (2012)
    - title: Formal Verification of Safety Properties for Ownership Authentication Transfer Protocol
    - what: Models a six-message ownership-transfer protocol for a ubiquitous-computing device in CSP, expresses secrecy and authentication as CTL properties, and checks the finite-state model with NuSMV in the presence of an intruder. The protocol uses a central key server, old/new-owner nonces, a ticket, ownership-transfer confirmation and a final temporary ID. CSP processes define OldOwner, NewOwner and the server, while secrecy is marked with Claim Secret and authentication with Running/Commit events. The NuSMV model checks five concrete constraints: three intended owner properties hold, while an intruder sending UserB or UserA credentials is rejected and yields counterexamples. The paper explains each message and trace, including the fairness motivation for requiring the seller's second confirmation.
    - number: six protocol steps M1–M6
    - number: five specifications in Table 2
    - number: three intended-owner cases true
    - number: two intruder cases false
    - limits: The price is agreed before transfer, users are physically proximate, and the whole protocol runs on the device for sale; encryption/public-key and nonce handling are idealized. NuSMV checks a finite abstraction with finite data types, so the results establish only the modeled protocol and claims, not implementation security, cryptographic soundness, privacy of residual data, or liveness. The paper reports no state-space size, runtime, parameter sweep, or independent attack analysis beyond the five formulas.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- Uppaal decentralized traffic monitoring (2012)
    - title: A Case Study on Formal Verification of Self-Adaptive Behaviors in a Decentralized System
    - what: Models and verifies a decentralized traffic-monitoring system in Uppaal. Intelligent cameras form and split organizations as traffic changes, while a self-healing subsystem detects silent camera failures, elects replacement masters and restores degraded monitoring. Timed automata model environment, camera agents, organization middleware and repair controllers; TCTL-like Uppaal queries express invariants, flexibility and robustness. The case study gives concrete environment models for car arrivals, camera viewing ranges, congestion-triggered organization merges/splits and injected failures. It verifies invariant/deadlock properties plus flexibility and robustness properties F1/F2 and R1–R4, including failure detection, reorganization, continued traffic monitoring and neighbor restoration. The authors deliberately restrict failure injection with a virtual environment to control state explosion, then reuse verified models as proposed inputs for model-based testing and runtime diagnosis. Verification time is measured as camera count grows and diagnostic traces are used to inspect violations.
    - number: I1 verification grew from 1.2s for 6 cameras to 197.3s for 60
    - number: deadlock I2 grew from 7.2s to 1330.6s over the same sizes
    - number: flexibility/robustness properties showed quasi-linear growth in the reported camera range
    - number: all specified properties were satisfied in the final model
    - limits: The model abstracts the implementation into finite timed automata and uses a restricted virtual environment; arbitrary environments and random failures are avoided because of state-space explosion. Silent failures are detected by timeout-based ping/echo, and recovery guarantees apply to the modeled roles, communication channels and scenarios. The study verifies a model, not the prototype Java implementation; model-based testing is proposed future work.
    - how much was read: most of it read

    - title: Reduction for Structured Concurrent Programs
    - what: The paper extends reduction to procedures and parallel composition in Civl. Reduction treats a group of small concurrent steps as one atomic step.
    - number: Cases: Snapshot 119 total / 82 impl+spec lines 0.4 s; ABD register 389/206 1.4 s; FLASH coherence 608/401 5 s; 2PC 146/111 1.8 s
    - number: About 50 further examples
    - limits: Some side conditions were checked by hand (Section 7). They are that left-mover procedures terminate and that right-mover statements cannot fail.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://civl-verifier.github.io/papers/esop2026.pdf); [notes: Civl and inductive sequentialization](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_4_invariant_inference_benchmarks.md); [notes: S17](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_9_formulation_methods.md)
    - record id: `civl-reduction-esop-2026-2026`
- Corten (2026)
    - title: Corten - Foundational Verification of Rust Programs
    - what: A framework in the Rocq proof assistant for proving Rust programs correct with separation logic (a logic for reasoning about who owns which memory). It defines Rust's behavior on the compiler's typed high-level IR so proof goals look like Rust source, and adds tactics that follow the code's shape.
    - number: Automated proofs are about 2x to 4x shorter than proofs done directly against the semantics, on a synthetic test suite
    - number: Buddy allocator case study: about 300 lines of Rust, about 2800 lines of Rocq (about 250 spec, about 800 general Rust reasoning, about 1400 free-list lemmas)
    - number: Specifications about 2x the size of the code; automated proofs 2x to 4x the Rust size
    - limits: Soundness of the semantic axioms is proved only for the pure and heap-statement fragments so far; the rest is itemized in an appendix. Specs of Rust core functions used by the case study are not proved. Single-threaded in practice (concurrency inherited from Iris but not yet exercised), traits only partly handled, no labeled break/continue. Allocator spec covers memory safety, not allocation quality.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04372); [notes: Corten (2026)](claude_readings.md)
    - record id: `corten-2026`
- I3DP (2026)
    - title: I3DP: Neuro-Symbolic Inductive Invariant Inference for Distributed Protocols
    - what: I3DP takes a TLA+ model and a safety goal. TLC screens candidate invariants (facts meant to hold in every reachable state). Apalache checks that each one stays true after every step. An LLM proposes stronger candidates. TLAPS, a proof checker, checks proofs that hold without a size bound. The notes know it only through its public artifact.
    - number: Artifact README records 29/29 passed in replay (not run by the reviewers)
    - number: artifact README records 29/29 replay passed (not rerun by notes)
    - limits: The paper's full text was not accessible. It covers safety of the model only, not running code or liveness. It has the same authors and benchmarks as IC3Syn (2605.24619) and is apparently its successor, but this is not confirmed. So it is not an independent evaluation.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://doi.org/10.1145/3830418.3843876); [notes: I3DP: Neuro-Symbolic Inductive Invariant Inference for Distributed Pro](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_18_invariant_agents.md); [notes: I3DP: Neuro-Symbolic Inductive Invariant Inference for Distributed Pro](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_11_second_pass_agents_invariants.md)
    - record id: `i3dp-2026`
- Lion (2026)
    - title: Lion: Modular Verification of Async Runtime Liveness
    - what: Lion is a Tokio-like async runtime written in Rust and verified in Verus. Its top theorem says that, under stated assumptions, a spawned task is eventually polled to completion. The liveness claim does not use temporal operators. Instead it is a bound on how long a task waits, under a filter on what the environment may do.
    - number: 3,393 lines executable, 39,648 lines spec/proof, 507 trusted (about 11.7 to 1)
    - number: 126 external_body items in the trusted base
    - number: Full verification run takes a few minutes
    - number: At least 95% of unverified-runtime throughput on nine workloads (claim)
    - limits: No machine-checked proof shows that the compiled runtime matches the model. The SOSP paper is not public, so the notes read the tech report and the repo in part.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://github.com/stonysystems/lion); notes outside this site: `/ssd1/sichangheagent/verus_distributed/lit_a/findings2_liveness.md`; [notes: Lion: Modular Verification of Async Runtime Liveness](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_1_verified_implementations.md); [notes: Newly found 2026 Verus liveness: Lion](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_2_liveness.md)
    - record id: `lion-2026`
- MachCSL (2026)
    - title: xv6 on RISC-V with AI agents (MachCSL)
    - what: Agents proved an xv6 operating-system kernel on RISC-V in Rocq, against a top-level statement: typing on the console prints only the typed text. Humans laid out the plan and designed the intermediate specs. Agents did the proofs.
    - number: 93 days; 1,479,165 lines of Rocq (294,402 generated) for 6,593 lines of xv6
    - number: 474 sessions, 2,620 sub-agent runs, 5,889 human prompts (median 13 words)
    - number: 2,062 agent-hours, 1,205 wall-clock hours, 226.9M output tokens, under $3,000 in subscriptions
    - number: Ten xv6 bugs and one Sail bug found; median kernel-update re-proof 35 agent-minutes

- Tulip (2026)
    - title: Verifying a high-performance distributed transaction system using permissioned state machines
    - what: Tulip is a distributed transaction system. It comes with a machine-checked proof that the implementation meets a spec identical to that of a local transaction system that is strictly serializable. The method combines TLA-style protocol invariants (facts that stay true in every reachable state) with separation-logic permissions.
    - number: about 4,000 lines of Go, about 42,000 lines of Rocq (about 11x)
    - number: 10 module types
    - number: bugs caught: transactional read, lock release, TAPIR timestamp inversion
    - number: 3,956 lines of Go code (abstract)
    - limits: The spec does not capture liveness, and the authors did not explore whether the method applies to liveness. The PDF does not name the venue.

    - what: The paper writes four concurrency anomalies of shared memory between LLM agents in TLA+, checked with TLC. It verifies Rust detectors and runtimes in a Verus crate.
    - number: 274 Verus obligations (zero assume, zero admit)
    - number: 24 obligations for the spec-implementation chain; refinement obligations 31 (pessimistic), 18 (SSI projection)
    - number: 274 Verus proof obligations

It covers safety only, and the authors state that liveness is a gap. It has a single author and the results are self-reported. The notes read about 25% of the paper.

- 3SF model checking (2025)
    - title: Technical Report: Exploring Automatic Model-Checking of the Ethereum specification
    - what: The authors translate the Python spec of Ethereum's 3-slot-finality (3SF) consensus protocol into TLA+ and use the Apalache model checker to test Accountable Safety: if two conflicting chains are both finalized, at least one third of validators can be shown to have broken a voting rule. They then add hand-made abstractions and re-encode the problem in SMT (CVC5) and Alloy to check larger instances.
    - number: Direct translation (Spec2): checking Accountable Safety timed out after more than 40 hours (Table 1)
    - number: Alloy with Kissat: Accountable Safety holds for up to 6 blocks; e.g. 5 blocks, 25 checkpoints, 12 votes in 234 s (Table 5)
    - number: CVC5: verified up to 5 blocks with 5 checkpoints (1h 40m) or 3 blocks with 6 checkpoints (74h) (Table 4)
    - number: A 7-block Alloy run went over 16 days without finishing
    - limits: Only the finality gadget and only Accountable Safety, on small bounded instances (at most 7 checkpoints and 24 votes); every validator has stake 1. The hand-made abstractions are not proved equal to the Python spec. Appendix A (translation rules) and B (soundness proofs) were skimmed, not read closely.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.07958); [notes: ## Exploring Automatic Model-Checking of the Ethereum specification (2](claude_readings.md); notes outside this site: `/ssd1/sichangheagent/verus_distributed/lit_a/findings2_leads.md`
    - record id: `3sf-model-checking-2025`
- A Refinement Methodology for Distributed Programs in Rust (precursor: Refinement Proofs in Rust Using Ghost Locks) (2025)
    - what: Proves that Rust distributed programs match a simpler model of what they should do (a refinement proof), using the Prusti verifier. The precursor paper also proves liveness (something good eventually happens). It writes those proofs as ghost code (code used only for proving, never run), using a direct encoding of LTL (a logic for statements about time). Cases include a simplified Memcached.
    - limits: The review notes could not get the final OOPSLA PDF and read the precursor instead. The verifier is Prusti, not Verus. The review notes do not know whether the final version keeps the liveness part.

- Anvil thesis (2025)
    - title: Building and Verifying Cloud Infrastructure Systems with State-Centric Reasoning (PhD dissertation)
    - what: The thesis proves liveness of Kubernetes controllers in Verus, using the Anvil framework. The human writes one general liveness formula, "eventually stable reconciliation": if the desired state stops changing, the cluster eventually matches it and stays that way. The proof splits into two parts: the environment becomes stable, and the controller reaches the desired state.
    - number: about two person-months to verify the ZooKeeper controller, about two person-weeks for each of the other two
    - number: each controller takes around 2.5 person-months to implement and verify; proof-to-code ratio 4.5 to 7.4
    - number: ZooKeeper: executable 1,134 lines, proof 8,352 (ESR 7,245), trusted 950; RabbitMQ: executable 1,598, proof 7,228, trusted 548
    - number: framework: 5353 lines of reusable lemmas, 7817 lines of trusted code; 70+ temporal-rule lemmas, 60 Kubernetes-environment lemmas
    - limits: The controller must be a state machine whose actions are atomic (they cannot be interrupted). The proof trusts the environment model, the shim layer, external APIs, the verifier, the compiler and the OS. After verification, testing still found one bug. It came from an incomplete spec of a trusted ZooKeeper API. About 40% of the 111 pages were read.
    - how much was read: parts read

- Clock-SI TLA+ (2025)
    - title: Formal modelling and analysis of Clock-SI with TLA+
    - what: A master's thesis that models the Clock-SI protocol (snapshot isolation for a partitioned database using loosely synchronized clocks) in TLA+ in two versions, a detailed one and a simpler one, and uses the TLC model checker to test that the protocol gives Snapshot Isolation, stated with a client-centric specification.
    - number: Abstract model, Snapshot Isolation check: 2 keys, 2 partitions, 2 txns 3 s; 3 txns 3 min 28 s; 3 keys, 2 partitions, 3 txns 11 h 23 min 20 s; 3 keys, 3 partitions, 3 txns timed out (Table 10)
    - number: Detailed model: 3 keys, 2 partitions, 2 txns 21 min; most larger cases timed out (Table 9)
    - number: Symmetry and view features cut one 2-key, 2-partition, 3-txn state-space run from 2 h 54 min to 2 min 2 s (Table 8)
    - number: Machine: i7-7700HQ, 16 GB RAM; each time is the average of 3 runs; maximum runtime 12 hours
    - limits: Bounded to at most 3 transactions, keys, partitions; the target of 3/3/3 was not reached. The abstract model removes the message system, so it does not cover the real messaging. Only the safety property (Snapshot Isolation) is checked; no liveness. Chapters 2-3 (background) and the model listings in 4.1-4.2 were skimmed.

- Korg thesis (2025)
    - title: Verification and Attack Synthesis for Network Protocols
    - what: Proves properties of three network-protocol pieces with theorem provers (Karn's RTT algorithm and TCP's timeout rule in Ivy and ACL2s, and Go-Back-N efficiency in ACL2s), model-checks TCP, DCCP and SCTP handshakes in SPIN, then builds Korg, a tool that searches for attacker behaviour that breaks a given property.
    - number: Handshake properties: TCP 4 (phi1-phi4), SCTP 10 (gamma1-gamma10), DCCP has its own set
    - number: Korg asked for at most 10 attacks per property; default search depth 600,000, maximum 2,400,000
    - number: TCP phi3 (peers do not get stuck) took 72 min to 223 min per attacker model; other TCP properties took seconds
    - number: SCTP: one patched-vs-unpatched check; with the CVE-2021-3772 patch, Korg finds no off-path attack, which proves the patch closes it for the model
    - limits: Models are small by design to avoid state-space explosion; the handshake models are not linked to the Ivy/ACL2s models. Korg handles one centralized attacker, not coordinated multi-process attackers, and is exact only for the model, not the real code. Models took long to write by hand.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.01124); [notes: ## Von Hippel thesis on protocol verification and attack synthesis (20](claude_readings.md)

- PolySemist (2025)
    - title: Accelerating Protocol Synthesis and Detecting Unrealizability with Interpretation Reduction.
    - what: A tool that fills in the missing guards and updates of a half-written TLA+ distributed protocol (a sketch) so the protocol meets a safety or liveness property. It loops: the TLC model checker finds a counterexample, the tool rules out every candidate that shares it, and it skips candidates that behave the same on the states seen so far.
    - number: 7 benchmark protocols: 2PC, consensus, decentralized lock, lock server, sharded KV store, two reconfigurable Raft variants
    - number: faster than the previous tool Scythe in 160 of 171 realizable problems; total time about 9,000 s vs 32,000 s on those both solve
    - number: whole distributed-lock protocol (6 expressions over 2 actions) synthesized in 125 s; Scythe timed out at 1 hour
    - number: detected 'no protocol exists' in 80 of 123 unrealizable problems vs 16 for Scythe (easy 46/50, medium 21/35, hard 13/38)
    - limits: A human supplies the sketch (state variables, actions, hole grammars) and the property; results hold only for the chosen finite parameters (e.g. number of nodes), and the all-parameters step is left to TLAPS proofs from prior work. Termination is guaranteed only when each hole grammar has finitely many expressions up to interpretation equivalence.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.14585); [notes: ## PolySemist: protocol synthesis by sketching (2025)](claude_readings.md); notes outside this site: `lit_snowball/notes_coci6.md`
    - record id: `polysemist-2025`
- Shipwright (2025)
    - title: Shipwright: Proving liveness of distributed systems with Byzantine participants
    - what: A Dafny framework. A node is written as event handlers and compiled to Go. A refinement proof shows that the real handlers, running over a network model that eventually stabilizes, give safety and liveness. The case study is PBFT agreeing on one log entry, with infinitely many views.
    - number: Shipwright 13,357 lines (1,351 trusted); PBFT implementation 31,138 lines (266 trusted)
    - number: total 1,617 trusted, 42,878 untrusted, 44,495 lines
    - number: proof found three liveness bugs (bad new-view signatures, wrong certificate counting, timer started too early)
    - number: implementation 1888 lines (Figure 11, PDF p.11)
    - limits: It assumes partial synchrony (the network eventually stabilizes), an unverified Go runtime, and unforgeable signatures. It covers only one PBFT iteration. Reply messages to clients are not in the top-level theorem. Some refinements are unfinished. Person-time is not given.

- Yao thesis (DistAI, DuoAI, LVR) (2024)
    - title: Automated Verification of Safety and Liveness Properties for Distributed Protocols (PhD thesis)
    - what: It shows that liveness can be reduced to a list of simpler safety properties, which invariant-inference tools often prove automatically. One task is left: synthesize a ranking function (a number that decreases toward the goal) to prove termination. It was applied to 8 protocols, including Paxos. It also covers DistAI and DuoAI.
    - number: 37 of 40 generated safety properties are always true, inductive, or get an invariant automatically; 3 found manually in a few hours each
    - number: every Z3 query to synthesize a ranking function finished in under a second
    - number: manual effort 128 and 165 lines versus IronFleet's 2,093 and 7,869 lines of Dafny (16x and 47x less)
    - number: DuoAI covers 27 protocols and solves Paxos more than two orders of magnitude faster than any alternative
    - limits: It handles only "eventually" properties, not bounded time. Ranking functions are polynomials only. It works on protocol-level models, not code. It assumes fair scheduling, minimum packet delivery and a contention-free Paxos round. Part of the line-count gap is Dafny versus mypyvy. The notes' author read about 20% of the 137 pages.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://academiccommons.columbia.edu/doi/10.7916/fexf-nt82/download); notes outside this site: `/ssd1/sichangheagent/verus_distributed/lit_a/findings2_google.md`
    - record id: `yao-thesis-distai-duoai-lvr-2024`
- A Case Study on Parametric Verification of Failure Detectors (2023)
    - what: The authors model the Chandra-Toueg failure detector (a protocol that guesses which machines have crashed) in TLA+, Ivy and counter automata, assuming partial synchrony (the network is unpredictable at first, then becomes well-behaved). They prove liveness (something good eventually happens) by hand, turning it into a safety proof (nothing bad happens).
    - limits: The conversion from liveness to safety is done by hand. Ivy's automatic conversion was not used.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2112.08826); notes outside this site: `/ssd1/sichangheagent/verus_distributed/lit_a/findings2_liveness.md`

- ByMC survey (2023)
    - title: Survey on Parameterized Verification with Threshold Automata and the Byzantine Model Checker
    - what: Protocols are modeled by counting messages and participants against thresholds. This lets the tool check safety, and some liveness, for any number of participants, under conditions like n>3t (n participants, at most t faulty).
    - number: Table 4: 13 asynchronous/randomized algorithms (FRB, STRB, ABA, NBACG, NBACR, CBC, CF1S, C1CS, BOSCO, Ben-Or, RABC, kSet, RS-BOSCO)
    - number: Table 6: 16 synchronous cases, 14 verified; HybridQueen and kSetOmit have no successful check
    - limits: People still supply the models and assumptions. Termination of randomized protocols holds only against adversaries that act in rounds. The Tendermint claim covers one round only. The synchronous checker is a separate prototype.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2011.14789); [notes: ByMC and threshold automata](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_4_invariant_inference_benchmarks.md)

- IronSync (2023)
    - title: Sharding the State Machine: Automated Modular Reasoning for Complex Concurrent Systems
    - what: It connects ownership reasoning (who may access what) for thread-local code to a global transition model. Cases: a NUMA node-replication library, and SplinterCache, a concurrent page cache.
    - number: 2747 lines of non-ghost implementation plus 316 of shared library, for all four concurrent examples (§7.1, PDF p.12)
    - limits: It covers shared-memory systems, not networking. It does not verify liveness, termination or freedom from deadlock. It trusts the framework's axioms and the memory model.

- Proving Refinement in a Rust Verifier (MSc thesis) (2023)
    - what: A way to prove that a Rust program, checked by Prusti, refines a TLA+ model (every step of the program matches a step of the model). The model state is ghost state (proof-only state) behind a "ghost lock". Each implementation step takes the lock to perform one model step.
    - number: Paxos acceptor phases: 112 model / 132 implementation / 152 proof lines (3.5 min)
    - number: lock-free hash set: 123 / 57 / 376 lines (3 min)
    - limits: It covers safety only. Prusti lacks support for ghost code. The translation from TLA+ is manual and not implemented as automatic. The notes' author read about 35%.

- Adore (2022)
    - title: Adore: Atomic Distributed Objects with Certified Reconfiguration
    - what: Adore describes a distributed protocol's state at a high level. It tracks which parts are committed and which are not, and hides packet details. A Coq proof shows that a Raft-like protocol, SRaft, correctly implements Adore (a refinement). Adore covers several ways of changing cluster membership, chosen by parameters.
    - number: about 13.8k Coq lines overall; 2.5k for SRaft-to-Adore refinement
    - number: 2 weeks for CADO safety plus another 3 for reconfiguration, five person-weeks, abstract safety only (§7, PDF p.13)
    - number: 13.8K lines for the full Raft development
    - limits: The proof is about the protocol, not application code. It makes no claim that the system eventually makes progress or stays available.

- Applying Formal Verification to Microkernel IPC at Meta (2022)
    - what: Meta proved two lock-free queues, used for message passing between processes in an OS under development, using Iris and Coq. The proofs cover verified pseudocode of the algorithm, not the C code.

- Op-based CRDTs (2022)
    - title: Modular Verification of Op-Based CRDTs in Separation Logic
    - what: A reliable causal broadcast library and OpLib, so that a data type can be verified without redoing the proofs about packets and threads. Twelve examples: counters, sets, registers, combinators, composed maps.

- Sift (2022)
    - title: Sift: Using Refinement-guided Automation to Verify Complex Distributed Systems
    - what: Starting from a specification, an implementation model and intermediate layers, it turns each layer-to-layer obligation into a safety problem. It infers invariants with IC3PO and generates C++ with Ivy.
    - number: six cases: leader election, distributed lock, two-phase commit, sharded hash table, Raft, Multi-Paxos
    - number: manual work under five minutes for the three small cases, one person-month for Raft, three person-weeks for Multi-Paxos
    - number: Raft proof effort 1 person-month
    - number: MultiPaxos effort 3 person-weeks

    - title: Distributed Causal Memory: Modular Specification and Verification in Higher-Order Distributed Separation Logic
    - what: Specifies a causally consistent database (one where an effect is never seen before its cause). Servers and clients are proved separately, and a session manager adds guarantees for each client on top.

    - title: The Civl Verifier
    - what: Describes a static verifier for concurrent programs. It proves a program in layers, where each layer is shown to match the one above. It uses checks for interference between threads, a step-merging technique called reduction, and permissions. Case studies include garbage collection, Paxos and locks.

- eXtreme Modelling (2020)
    - title: eXtreme Modelling in Practice
    - what: MongoDB compared two ways to link a model to code. One is model-based trace checking (MBTC), which checks whether logged runs fit the model. The other is model-based test-case generation (MBTCG). Trace checking of replication was impractical. For Realm Sync's operational-transformation rules, a Go generator turned TLC states into C++ tests.
    - number: Realm Sync table (§5.2): 795 TLA+ lines, 755 Go lines, 4 weeks total effort
    - number: Abandoned trace checking after 10 weeks
    - number: 4913 generated tests reached 100% branch coverage vs 21% handwritten and 92% AFL fuzzer
    - limits: Trace checking of replication struggled with the level of abstraction, concurrency, incomplete observations, and keeping code and model in sync. The generated tests cover local merge behavior, not the whole distributed product.

    - title: A Formally Verified Protocol for Log Replication with Byzantine Fault Tolerance
    - what: A log replication protocol that runs in lock-step rounds (the Heard-Of model), uses signatures, and tolerates Byzantine faults. Its proof in Isabelle/HOL includes liveness.
    - number: About 300 lines for the protocol model and 1000 lines for the proofs
    - number: IronFleet took about 3.7 person-years (the paper's figure)

- Apalache (2019)
    - title: Apalache: temporal properties tutorial, feature table and TLA+ model checking made symbolic
    - what: Apalache is a model checker for TLA+ that works on symbols instead of listing states, using an SMT solver. It checks state, action and trace invariants, inductive invariants (a property that holds at the start and is preserved by every step) and temporal properties.
    - limits: Apalache parses ASSUME but does not pass it to the solver. It does not use THEOREM. Its bounded checks do not prove properties for unbounded runs. The authors did not read the original paper.

- HotStuff (2019)
    - title: HotStuff: BFT Consensus in the Lens of Blockchain
    - what: HotStuff is BFT consensus (it tolerates arbitrarily faulty replicas) with n=3f+1 replicas, a known delay bound Δ and an unknown GST. If a correct leader stays in one view long enough after GST, Basic HotStuff decides. A Pacemaker keeps replicas and leaders in step.
    - limits: The argument is handwritten. The deployed Pacemaker is not machine-checked. The chained variant needs two consecutive correct leaders, and its liveness argument is omitted for brevity. Completing in a single view does not establish that this view is eventually selected (an inference).
    - how much was read: parts read

    - record id: `hotstuff-2019`
- Pretend Synchrony / Goolong (2019)
    - title: Pretend synchrony: synchronous verification of asynchronous distributed programs
    - what: Verifies Go distributed programs by automatically rewriting them into an equivalent synchronous program (messages become assignments), then checking ordinary verification conditions with the Z3 solver. Four case studies: two-phase commit, Raft leader election, single-decree Paxos, Multi-Paxos key-value store.
    - number: manual invariant annotations reduced by 6x versus Dafny
    - number: check time 1.73 s total vs 1455.84 s in Dafny for the three protocols
    - number: Paxos 504 lines of Go, 14 loop-invariant lines, 1.51 s; Dafny 72 invariant lines, 1141.35 s
    - number: Multi-Paxos KV: 847 Go lines, 1.64 s check, 118.5 req/ms, within 1.5-3x of unverified EPaxos code
    - limits: Safety only, no liveness. Needs structured loops, symmetric process ids, typed channels, pairwise communication and round non-interference; rules out Multi-Paxos stable-leader optimization, Stoppable Paxos and ring topologies such as Chord.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://doi.org/10.1145/3290372); [notes: Pretend Synchrony (2019)](claude_readings.md)

- Tendermint (2018)
    - title: The latest gossip on BFT consensus
    - what: The paper presents the Tendermint algorithm. It proves that, after GST, a favorable round arises from the proposer rotation, the growing timeouts and the gossip. Then all correct participants decide.
    - number: faulty voting power below one third of total

    - title: Velisarios: Byzantine Fault-Tolerant Protocols for Verified Distributed Systems
    - what: It proves agreement for an executable Coq implementation of PBFT: correct replicas return the same reply for the same client and timestamp. Code is extracted to OCaml with a networking and cryptography runtime.
    - number: PBFT evaluation uses desktops with 16 GB RAM and 8-core 3.40 GHz i7-6700 CPUs
    - number: Single-machine simulation: 1 million requests, 27.6 microseconds average for f=1 replies
    - number: Primary failure recovery took around 7 seconds
    - number: Compared response times with BFT-SMaRt for f=1–3

- ConsL cutoff (2017)
    - title: Cutoff Bounds for Consensus Algorithms
    - what: It describes round-based consensus algorithms in a small language, ConsL. It proves bounds on how many processes are enough to check. It translates the algorithms to Promela/LTL for the Spin model checker.
    - number: Table 2: 9 cases (Paxos, Paxos 3 rounds, Chandra-Toueg, an algorithm from Consensus Refined, 1/3-rule, coordinated uniform voting, simplified version, uniform voting variant, Ben-Or)
    - number: Cutoffs: 5 processes, except 1/3-rule at 7
    - number: Pages 217-237
    - limits: Progress claims keep the communication assumptions. Failed processes keep receiving, but their messages are suppressed. Ben-Or termination here is not termination with probability one against arbitrary adversaries. The notes did not recover the generated model download.

- Heard-Of (AFP) (2012)
    - title: Verifying Fault-Tolerant Distributed Algorithms in the Heard-Of Model
    - what: Isabelle proofs of six consensus algorithms in the Heard-Of model. Two ways of defining runs are linked by a reduction theorem. Termination of LastVoting is proved under a "communication predicate" (a condition on which messages arrive) instead of weak or strong fairness.

- PBFT (1999)
    - title: Practical Byzantine Fault Tolerance
    - what: Clients eventually get replies if at most (n-1)/3 replicas are faulty and message delays do not grow faster than the timeout forever. The proof raises view timeouts, gets correct replicas into the same view, and rotates primaries until a correct one has enough time.
    - limits: Read in the official HTML because the PDFs failed. The delay condition is specific to this paper. It is not the modern GST contract.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://usenix.org/publications/library/proceedings/osdi99/full_papers/castro/castro_html/castro.html); [notes: Consensus: where the assumptions enter the proof](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_2_liveness.md); [notes: PBFT](../distributed_verification_review_b/review_b_consensus_20261007.md)
    - record id: `pbft-1999`
- PVerifier
    - title: PVerifier (P language verification extensions)
    - what: It checks inductive proofs of designs written in the P language. Users supply assumptions and an inductive invariant. UCLID5 and an SMT solver check that the invariant holds initially, that each step preserves it, and that it implies the desired property. A two-phase-commit tutorial shows that if the protocol accepts, every participant preferred acceptance.
    - limits: The claim holds for the supplied P design under its assumptions. The 2PC tutorial proves no theorem that the protocol completes despite crashes. An internal verification of sharded transactions with snapshot isolation is reported by the author, and its details are unreleased.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://github.com/p-org/P/blob/master/Docs/docs/advanced/PVerifierLanguageExtensions/announcement.md); [notes: PVerifier](../distributed_verification_review_b/review_b_additional_tools_20261007.md)
    - record id: `pverifier`
- tla-rs
    - what: Ports the AutoMan workflow to Rust and Verus. In the RSL and Raft theorems the review notes inspected, finite behaviors of the distributed model are proved to refine an abstract spec. The Raft theorem maps behavior to an abstract behavior of the committed log.
    - limits: It covers safety correspondence only, with no theorem about eventual progress. Collection helpers are marked external_body, so their contracts are not checked against their bodies. The scheduler, unsafe syscall glue, identity and TLS are outside the body proofs. Verification counts and benchmarks are the authors' reports, which the review notes did not reproduce.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://github.com/stonysystems/tla-rs); [notes: tla-rs primary source audit](../distributed_verification_review_b/review_b_automan_20261007.md)

    - what: Describes Apalache, a symbolic TLA+ model checker using SMT, with bounded verification, randomized symbolic execution, and inductiveness checking. A Byzantine consensus case study shows safety and liveness-to-safety workflows and their cost.

    - what: This paper studies specifications and proofs of distributed algorithms when only message history variables are used, using the Basic Paxos and Multi-Paxos algorithms for distributed consensus as precise case studies. It also allows easier proofs to be developed by needing fewer invariants and facilitating proof derivations.

- Weak isolation (Aneris) (2025)
    - title: Reasoning about Weak Isolation Levels in Separation Logic
    - what: The paper writes transaction specs that do not depend on the implementation, for read-uncommitted, read-committed and snapshot isolation. It proves that known bad histories are prohibited. It verifies an in-memory, single-node MVCC key-value database, accessed over RPC, in OCaml/Aneris.
    - limits: It covers a single node, with no replication or crash recovery. The review did not verify the translator from source to AnerisLang or the runtime.
    - how much was read: reading depth not recorded
    - [source](https://iris-project.org/pdfs/2025-icfp-weak-isolations.pdf); [notes: Reasoning about Weak Isolation Levels in Separation Logic](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_1_verified_implementations.md)
    - record id: `weak-isolation-aneris-2025`
- AdoB (2024)
    - title: AdoB: Bridging Benign and Byzantine Consensus with Atomic Distributed Objects
    - what: The authors use Coq to prove safety and progress of a generic consensus object. They also prove that specs written at the network level are refinements of it (they behave as a more detailed version of the object). For GenJolteon, they also prove liveness at the network level. They extract it to runnable OCaml event handlers, using a handwritten shim. For Fast Paxos, they prove only safety and refinement.
    - number: abstract benign AdoB proof under one person-month
    - number: GenJolteon refinement/safety eight person-months; liveness two person-months
    - number: Fast Paxos safety/refinement one person-month
    - number: proof lines 17,000, 2,700, 2,000 (case-study discussion, PDF p.22)
    - limits: This does not verify the production Jolteon or Fast Paxos code. There is no network-level liveness proof for Fast Paxos.
    - how much was read: reading depth not recorded
    - [source](https://flint.cs.yale.edu/publications/adob.pdf); [notes: AdoB: Bridging Benign and Byzantine Consensus with Atomic Distributed ](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_1_verified_implementations.md)
    - record id: `adob-2024`
- Fairneris (2024)
    - title: Verifying Liveness Properties of Distributed Systems via Trace Refinement in Higher-Order Concurrent Separation Logic
    - what: Fairneris extends Aneris/Trillium to reason about progress in distributed systems. It reasons about code for the Stenning sliding-window protocol over a formal semantics resembling UDP. The proof that the implementation matches the model is machine-checked.
    - limits: The Stenning model's liveness theorem is only a proof on paper. So the transfer of its eventual-progress result to the implementation is not fully machine-checked. No runnable OCaml extraction was established, and the final venue is unknown.
    - how much was read: reading depth not recorded
    - [source](https://cpv.au.dk/pdfs/2024-fairneris.pdf); [notes: Verifying Liveness Properties of Distributed Systems via Trace Refinem](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_1_verified_implementations.md)
    - record id: `fairneris-2024`
- Aneris reliable channels (2023)
    - title: Verifying Reliable Network Components in a Distributed Separation Logic with Dependent Separation Protocols
    - what: The paper specifies the order and contents of messages over a reliable-channel abstraction built on unreliable UDP. It uses dependent protocols, which let each message's allowed form depend on earlier messages. The authors reuse the channel for RPC and for a leader/follower key-value service.
    - limits: It proves the code does the right thing under the modeled network. It does not claim that a failed peer eventually answers. No audited link from the model to the real runtime is given.
    - how much was read: reading depth not recorded
    - [source](https://iris-project.org/pdfs/2023-icfp-relcom.pdf); [notes: Verifying Reliable Network Components in a Distributed Separation Logi](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_1_verified_implementations.md)
    - record id: `aneris-reliable-channels-2023`
- StateLib (2023)
    - title: Modular Verification of State-Based CRDTs in Separation Logic
    - what: A reusable verified library for merging replica states. Each CRDT proof connects its lattice and merge function to the application's operations. Proves convergence and functional correctness of several example CRDTs.
    - limits: The proof is over AnerisLang. Running on OCaml is an experiment (§4.2), not a verified compiler or runtime. No theorem about delivery or termination.
    - how much was read: reading depth not recorded
    - [source](https://iris-project.org/pdfs/2023-ecoop-crdts.pdf); [notes: Modular Verification of State-Based CRDTs in Separation Logic](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_1_verified_implementations.md)
    - record id: `statelib-2023`
- ADO (2021)
    - title: Much ADO about Failures: A Fault-Aware Model for Compositional Verification of Strongly Consistent Distributed Systems
    - what: The ADO interface shows chosen failure cases to the user. It does not pretend that each operation either fully succeeds or does nothing. The authors use Coq and CCAL to prove that distributed C implementations (for example Multi-Paxos), and the clients built on them, match their abstract specs. The proofs sit on top of the verified CertiKOS.
    - number: multi-Paxos: 2.6K lines of C and 43.9K lines of functional correctness proofs (§6, PDF p.23)
    - limits: The results say nothing about progress (that the system eventually finishes its work). One example assumes termination. That assumption is not a proof of progress.
    - how much was read: reading depth not recorded

    - record id: `ado-2021`
- ReSync (2020)
    - title: Programming at the Edge of Synchrony
    - what: ReSync lets a process receive messages bit by bit within a round. It also lets each protocol set its own policy for moving to the next round. The paper establishes a round-switch abstraction and evaluates runnable consensus implementations.
    - limits: The evaluated applications are not thereby fully proved.
    - how much was read: reading depth not recorded
    - [source](https://dzufferey.github.io/files/2020_Programming_at_the_Edge_of_Synchrony.pdf); [notes: Programming at the Edge of Synchrony](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_1_verified_implementations.md)
    - record id: `resync-2020`
- Perennial (2019)
    - title: Verifying concurrent, crash-safe systems with Perennial
    - what: Perennial adds reasoning about crashes and recovery to concurrent separation logic. It separates specs for ordinary calls from specs for recovery, and durable ownership from volatile ownership. Examples are a crash-safe mail system and local replicated disks.
    - limits: It covers replication across local disks only, not a replication protocol over a network.
    - how much was read: reading depth not recorded
    - [source](https://www.chajed.io/papers/perennial:sosp2019.pdf); [notes: Verifying concurrent, crash-safe systems with Perennial](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_1_verified_implementations.md)
    - record id: `perennial-2019`
- WormSpace (2019)
    - title: WormSpace: A Modular Foundation for Simple, Verifiable Distributed Systems
    - what: Wormspace has a write-once-register API. Clients can reason about immutable agreed values instead of the coordination protocol. Certified concurrent abstraction layers prove that C code refines the API, and links it to the verified CertiKOS.
    - number: 6 person-months core verification (4.5 functional correctness, 1.5 ghost layer) and 5 person-weeks application/linking proofs (§6, PDF p.10)
    - number: 108K verification lines
    - limits: Chain-replication registers and reconfiguration are outside the verified part. There is no availability proof.
    - how much was read: reading depth not recorded

- PSync (2016)
    - title: PSync: A Partially Synchronous Language for Fault-Tolerant Distributed Algorithms
    - what: PSync algorithms are written in communication rounds, with stated assumptions about which rounds are good. A Scala runtime runs them. SMT checks the user's invariants and progress conditions under lockstep round semantics (all nodes advance round by round together). A reduction connects these to partially synchronous executions.
    - number: LastVoting algorithm is 89 lines (Table 2, PDF p.13)
    - limits: The runtime cannot be written in PSync's round language. So the reduction theorem does not verify every statement of the runtime.
    - how much was read: reading depth not recorded
